❯ Do these three steps in order, in this session. Make no spec or ADR change    
  without asking me first, and stop to ask if a step needs a decision of mine.  
                                                                                
  Step 1, runbooks.                                                             
  Write and extend the runbooks in docs/runbooks/, one file each, with this     
  structure: symptoms and alerts, impact, diagnosis (exact queries, commands    
  and metrics from this repo), step-by-step mitigation, verification, and       
  follow-up. Use exactly these files, which docs/observability.md already       
  links, and keep the existing names, since other documents link them:          
  - deploy-and-migrate.md (exists): deploy and rollback.                        
  - database.md (new): RDS Multi-AZ failover and what clients see, and the      
  alarms rds-cpu, rds-storage, rds-connections and rds-proxy-pinned.            
  - capacity.md (new): the alarms ecs-cpu, ecs-memory, cache-memory and         
  alb-healthy-targets.                                                          
  - timeouts-and-503.md (exists): database connection pool exhaustion, and the  
  alarms alb-5xx and alb-target-response-time-p99.                              
  - rate-limits.md (exists): Redis down with rate limiting failing open, and    
  the alarm waf-blocked-requests.                                               
  - reconciliation.md (exists): a ledger invariant violation.                   
  - idempotency-in-progress.md (new): a spike of 409 request-in-progress.       
  - retry-storm.md (new): a deadlock or serialization retry storm.              
  - secret-rotation.md (new): rotating the JWT secret and the database          
  credentials without downtime.                                                 
  - compromised-account.md (new): a suspected compromised account: freeze,      
  investigate, reverse.                                                         
  - shutdown.md and idempotency-cleanup.md (exist): bring them to the same      
  structure.                                                                    
  Each runbook names the alert in docs/observability.md that triggers it; add   
  the alert there if it is missing. Update docs/runbooks/README.md to index     
  them all and link each one to its alert. Then run npm run docs:check: every   
  link must resolve.                                                            
                                                                                
  Step 2, documentation check.                                                  
  Run npm run docs:check, which the docs job of CI runs (added with the README  
  in 14.1), and fix every diagram or link that fails.                           
  Then read README.md and every document in docs/ (except docs/ai/) top to      
  bottom as a newcomer would: check that every link resolves and that nothing   
  is missing, unclear, outdated or contradicting the code, and fix it.          
                                                                                
  Step 3, the AI log and my texts.                                              
  Update docs/ai/README.md so every transcript in docs/ai/transcripts/ has a    
  row in its table (#, Date, Transcript, Summary), in order, with a one-line    
  summary of what each session produced. Do not edit the transcripts or         
  docs/ai/00-planning.md.                                                       
  Then add my own texts below exactly as written, changing nothing but turning  
  each ADR number into a link to its file in docs/adr/:                         
                                                                                
  1. In README.md, the body of the section "How this was built", keeping its    
  link to docs/ai/:                                                             
                                                                                
  I built this service with AI, under a spec-first process. I set the scope and 
  the rules, decided the architecture and approved every change to a spec.      
  Claude Code implemented each phase from the specs, ADRs and plans in this     
  repository, and an independent review agent audited every phase before it was 
  merged. Every session's prompts and responses are in docs/ai/, which also     
  explains what the AI did and what I decided.                                  
                                                                                
  2. In docs/ai/README.md, a section "What the AI did and what I decided":      
                                                                                
  **How I worked.** I treated the AI as the implementation team and kept the    
  decisions. Each phase started from a written spec with acceptance criteria,   
  and from an ADR for each significant choice. Claude Code implemented it       
  test-first under the rules of AGENTS.md. An independent agent that had not    
  seen the session audited the result, and /ship committed it only after the    
  quality gates passed. Any change to an approved spec needed my approval,      
  recorded with its date in section 11 of plan 000.                             
                                                                                
  **What the AI did.**                                                          
  - Claude Code wrote the code, the tests, the Terraform, the CI pipeline and   
  the documentation, following the specs.                                       
  - The /audit agent reviewed each phase against the specs and ADRs; I decided  
  which findings were fixed before shipping.                                    
  - A separate Claude chat helped me plan the phases, draft the prompts I gave  
  Claude Code (all of them are in the transcripts), and review each result and  
  each audit before I approved it.                                              
                                                                                
  **What I decided.** The main decisions:                                       
  - PostgreSQL as the only source of truth, although the challenge allowed      
  in-memory storage: money needs transactions, row locks and constraints shared 
  by several replicas (ADR-0005).                                               
  - A modular monolith with hexagonal modules instead of microservices: one     
  transactional boundary for money, with seams that allow a later split         
  (ADR-0002, ADR-0003).                                                         
  - A double-entry ledger in integer minor units, and idempotency inside the    
  same transaction as the movement (ADR-0006, ADR-0009).                        
  - Two replicas behind a load balancer from the start, so that concurrency and 
  retries are tested across processes, not assumed.                             
  - AWS on ECS Fargate with RDS, described in Terraform that CI validates       
  against policies and that this repository never applies (ADR-0014, ADR-0015). 
  - Dashboards and error reporting as opt-in extras, off by default, and no     
  product-analytics tool for a money service (ADR-0023). No demo UI, to spend   
  the time on the backend.                                                      
                                                                                
  **How I checked.** Beyond the gates and the audits, I exercised the running   
  stack by hand (Swagger UI, curl scenarios, Grafana) and reviewed every result 
  with the advisor chat before approving it. When CI failed, I asked for the    
  cause before the fix: for example, the load test's 503s on a two-CPU CI       
  runner came from capacity, so CI runs it at 100 requests per second while the 
  local benchmark stays at 200.

         +        | Means

         +ith an exit code other than 0; the pipeline stops before the se
         +rvice changes.         |

         +ability.md#alerts-without-an-alarm)) | The ECS circuit breaker

      14 +                                                               
      15 +## Impact                                                      
      16 +                                                               
      17 +- **A failed migration.** None for clients: the old version kee
         +ps serving on the schema as it was before the failed migration,
         + which runs in its own transaction. New code is not deployed.  
      18 +- **A deployment rolled back by the circuit breaker.** None for
         + clients: `deployment_minimum_healthy_percent` 100 keeps the ol
         +d tasks serving until new ones are healthy, and the new ones ne
         +ver were.                                                      
      19 +- **A bad version that is live.** Clients get its errors until 
         +it is rolled back. Money stays correct: every movement is one t
         +ransaction, whatever the version.                              
      20 +                                                               
      21 +During every deployment, old and new versions serve side by sid
         +e against one schema. Migrations only expand, so both work; old
         + tasks drain before they stop, so no request is cut off ([shutd
         +own](shutdown.md)).                                            
      22 +                                                               
      23 +## Diagnosis                                                   
      24 +                                                               
      25 +1. The migration task's exit code and its one-line reason, with
         + the SQLSTATE or the variable at fault, never a password, in th
         +e log group `/scf/migrate`:                                    
      26 +                                                               
      27 +   ```sh                                                       
      28 +   aws ecs describe-tasks --cluster <cluster> --tasks <task-arn
         +> \                                                            
      29 +     --query 'tasks[0].{exitCode: containers[0].exitCode, reaso
         +n: stoppedReason}'                                             
      30 +   aws logs tail /scf/migrate --since 1h                       
      31 +   ```                                                         
      32 +                                                               
      33 +2. The deployment's state and the service's events:            
      34 +                                                               
      35 +   ```sh                                                       
      36 +   aws ecs describe-services --cluster <cluster> --services scf
         +-api \                                                         
      37 +     --query 'services[0].{deployments: deployments[].{status: 
         +status, rollout: rolloutState, reason: rolloutStateReason, task
         +Definition: taskDefinition}, events: events[:10].message}'     
      38 +   ```                                                         
      39 +                                                               
      40 +3. Why new tasks did not turn healthy: the stopped tasks' reaso
         +ns ([capacity](capacity.md#diagnosis)), and `invalid configurat
         +ion` or `startup failed` in `/scf/api`.                        
      41 +4. For a version that is live but wrong: the 5xx by cause in `/
         +scf/api` ([timeouts and 503](timeouts-and-503.md#diagnosis)), a
         +nd `request failed` lines for 500s.                            
      42 +                                                               
      43 +Locally, `docker compose up --build --wait` runs the one-shot `
         +migrate` job first; a failed migration stops there, keeps the r
         +eplicas down (DEP-AC03), and `docker compose logs migrate` name
         +s the failure.                                                 
      44 +                                                               
      45 +## Mitigation                                                  
      46 +                                                               
      47 +### Every deployment                                           
      48 +                                                               
      49 +The pipeline runs, in order, and stops at the first failure:   
      50 +

      52  2. Register the migration task definition of that tag: `terrafo

       9 -3. Run the migration task with `aws ecs run-task` in the privat

         ->`, then `aws ecs wait services-stable --cluster <cluster> --se

      54 +4. Apply with the new tag: `terraform apply -var image_tag=<tag

       1  # Runbook: timeouts and 503
       2  
       3 -What a 503 means, how to find its cause, and what to do (sectio
         -ns 1.1, 1.7 and 1.9 of [spec 007](../../specs/007-security-ops/
         -spec.md), [ADR-0019](../adr/0019-timeout-layers-and-rds-proxy.m
         -d), [ADR-0022](../adr/0022-request-timeout-answer-first-then-ro
         -ll-back.md)). A 503 is a transient overload, never a defect: it
         - is answered with `Retry-After: 1`, is never stored for idempot
         -ent replay, and leaves no committed effect, with the one except
         -ion of a commit already sent at the request timeout (below). A 
         -client retries the same request with the same `Idempotency-Key`
         - (section 1.5 of [spec 008](../../specs/008-deployment/spec.md)
         -).                                                             
       3 +What a 503 means, how to find its cause, and what to do, includ
         +ing when the database connection pool is exhausted (sections 1.
         +1, 1.7 and 1.9 of [spec 007](../../specs/007-security-ops/spec.
         +md), [ADR-0019](../adr/0019-timeout-layers-and-rds-proxy.md), [
         +ADR-0022](../adr/0022-request-timeout-answer-first-then-roll-ba
         +ck.md)). A 503 is a transient overload, never a defect: it is a
         +nswered with `Retry-After: 1`, is never stored for idempotent r
         +eplay, and leaves no committed effect, with the one exception o
         +f a commit already sent at the request timeout (below). A clien
         +t retries the same request with the same `Idempotency-Key` (sec
         +tion 1.5 of [spec 008](../../specs/008-deployment/spec.md)).

       5 +## Symptoms and alerts

      10 +| `scf-alb-target-response-time-p99` ([alarms](../observability

      12 +                                                               
      13 +Clients see 503 `/problems/service-unavailable` from a replica,
         + or 502, 503 or 504 `/problems/upstream-unavailable` from nginx
         + (the ALB's own are `text/html`). Each replica's 503 writes one
         + `warn` line, `service unavailable`, with its `cause`.         
      14 +                                                               
      15 +The other alarms that come with 503s have their own runbooks: t
         +he database's in [database](database.md), the tasks' and the he
         +althy targets' in [capacity](capacity.md).                     
      16 +                                                               
      17 +## Impact                                                      
      18 +                                                               
      19 +A request that answers 503 applied nothing, its key row include
         +d, so its retry with the same key runs it afresh (IDM-R17). The
         + exception is a 503 at `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` whose `COMMIT` was 
         +already sent: its outcome is unknown to the client until the re
         +try, which gets the stored response if it committed (SEC-R33, A
         +DR-0022). Money is never lost or applied twice; clients see lat
         +ency and retries.                                              
      20 +                                                               
      21 +### The timeout layers                                         
      22 +                                                               
      23  Each layer gives up before the layer outside it, so the innermo
          st layer that knows why answers (SYS-R35).
      24  
      25  | Layer, innermost first  | Setting                            
                                 | Default                  | Answer when
           it runs out                                             |
      26  | ----------------------- | -----------------------------------
          ---------------------- | ------------------------ | -----------
          -------------------------------------------------------- |
      27  | Account row lock        | `ACCOUNT_LOCK_TIMEOUT_MS`          
                                 | 2000 ms                  | 503 `/probl
          ems/service-unavailable`                                 |
      12 -| Idempotency key wait    | `IDEMPOTENCY_WAIT_TIMEOUT_MS`      
         -                       | 2000 ms                  | 409 `/probl
         -ems/request-in-progress`, not a 503                      |     
      28 +| Idempotency key wait    | `IDEMPOTENCY_WAIT_TIMEOUT_MS`      
         +                       | 2000 ms                  | 409 `/probl
         +ems/request-in-progress`, not a 503 ([idempotency in progress](
         +idempotency-in-progress.md)) |                                 
      29  | Database statement      | `statement_timeout` on the runtime 
          role                   | 5 s                      | 503 `/probl
          ems/service-unavailable` (SQLSTATE 57014)                |
      30  | Idle in transaction     | `idle_in_transaction_session_timeou
          t` on the runtime role | 10 s                     | the databas
          e ends the session; only a defect reaches it             |
      31  | Connection pool acquire | `DB_POOL_ACQUIRE_TIMEOUT_MS`       
                                 | 2000 ms                  | 503 `/probl
          ems/service-unavailable`                                 |
     ...
      36  
      37  The service's keep-alive timeout, 65 s, stays above the load ba
          lancer's upstream keep-alive, 60 s, so the service never closes
           a connection the load balancer is about to reuse (SEC-R34).

       92 +## Mitigation                                                 
       93  
       46 -- **Pool exhausted.** `scf_db_pool_connections{state="waiting"
          -}` stays above 0 and `scf_db_pool_acquire_timeouts_total` rise
          -s. Short bursts queue in arrival order and drain without a 5xx
          - (SEC-R38); a steady excess needs more capacity. Raising `DB_P
          -OOL_MAX` must keep replicas × (`DB_POOL_MAX` + 1) + 10 below t
          -he database's `max_connections` − `superuser_reserved_connecti
          -ons` in every deployment: 2 × 11 + 10 = 32 < 97 in `compose.ya
          -ml`; in AWS a deployment's surge included, `max_tasks` 6 × `de
          -ployment_maximum_percent` 200 / 100 = 12 tasks, 12 × 11 + 10 =
          - 142 < 197 with the parameter group's `max_connections` of 200
          -; the tasks connect to RDS Proxy, which uses at most 90% of th
          -e instance's connections (SEC-R36, DEP-R29). In AWS the Terraf
          -orm validation of `max_tasks` refuses any tfvars that breaks t
          -he budget, and the unit test of SEC-AC29 checks `compose.yaml`
          - and the Terraform's defaults.                                
       47 -- **Lock timeouts.** Many movements on the same account at onc
          -e: they queue on its row lock in ascending id order (ADR-0008)
          -. Occasional 503s under such contention are expected and retri
          -ed by the client. If they persist with low traffic, look for a
          - session holding a lock: `SELECT pid, state, xact_start, query
          - FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state <> 'idle' ORDER BY xact_sta
          -rt;`.                                                         
       48 -- **Retries exhausted.** `scf_transaction_retries_total` by `s
          -qlstate` shows which conflict. Ordered locking should make dea
          -dlocks rare; a sustained rise is a defect to report.          
       49 -- **Statement timeouts.** A slow statement: check the database
          -'s load (the alarm `scf-rds-cpu` in AWS) and `pg_stat_activity
          -`. Do not raise `statement_timeout`: it must stay above the lo
          -ck waits and below `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (SEC-R34), and it boun
          -ds the clean-up after a request timeout.                      
       50 -- **Request timeouts.** The request ran longer than its whole 
          -budget. Its transaction rolls back once the statement in fligh
          -t ends; if its `COMMIT` was already sent, the commit finishes 
          -after the 503, the outcome is unknown to the client, and a ret
          -ry with the same key gets the stored response (SEC-R33, SEC-AC
          -38). Nothing needs repair; locally `npm run reconcile` confirm
          -s it ([reconciliation](reconciliation.md)). It runs locally an
          -d in CI only: in AWS it has no task yet ([limitations](../depl
          -oyment/aws.md#limitations-and-follow-ups)).                   
       51 -- **Database down or failing over.** Every request needing the
          - database answers 503 and readiness answers 503; liveness stay
          -s 200, so neither Docker nor ECS replaces the replicas, and th
          -ey serve again once the database is back ([database failover](
          -../deployment/aws.md#database-failover)).                     
       94 +- **Pool exhausted.** `scf_db_pool_connections{state="waiting"
          +}` stays above 0 and `scf_db_pool_acquire_timeouts_total` rise
          +s. Short bursts queue in arrival order and drain without a 5xx
          + (SEC-R38); a steady excess needs more capacity. If connection

         -ent/spec.md) does not retry a 429; slowing down is the client's
         - decision.

      13 -## A client gets 429                                           
      13 +| Alert                                                        
         +                               | Fires when                    
         +                                                               
         +                  |                                            
      14 +| -------------------------------------------------------------
         +------------------------------ | ------------------------------
         +---------------------------------------------------------------
         +----------------- |                                            
      15 +| `scf-waf-blocked-requests` ([alarms](../observability.md#alar
         +ms))                           | AWS WAF blocked more than 1000
         + requests in 5 minutes, by the per-IP rate rule or by a managed
         + rule group        |                                           
      16 +| `redis-unavailable` ([alerts without an alarm](../observabili
         +ty.md#alerts-without-an-alarm)) | `scf_rate_limit_store_errors_
         +total` rises, or a replica logs `Redis unavailable: the per-use
         +r rate limit lets requests through` |                          
      17  
      18 +Clients report 429 `/problems/rate-limited`, or a 403 from WAF.
      19 +                                                               
      20 +## Impact                                                      
      21 +                                                               
      22 +- **A 429.** The request changed nothing and is not stored for 
         +idempotent replay: it can be sent again, with the same `Idempot
         +ency-Key`, once `Retry-After` has passed. The automatic retry p
         +olicy of section 1.5 of [spec 008](../../specs/008-deployment/s
         +pec.md) does not retry a 429; slowing down is the client's deci
         +sion.                                                          
      23 +- **A 403 from WAF.** A managed rule group blocked the request 
         +as malicious, or its body is above 8 KB; the service never saw 
         +it.                                                            
      24 +- **Redis down.** Redis holds only the per-user counters (SEC-R
         +07). When it is unreachable or does not answer within `REDIS_CO
         +MMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` (100 ms), the limit fails open: every request
         + is served as if under the limit, and money, idempotency and re
         +adiness are unaffected (SEC-R06). Each request waits at most 10
         +0 ms for the check. The per-IP limit still applies, at nginx or
         + WAF, so the exposure is one user sending more than 300 request
         +s per 10 s from addresses below the per-IP limit.              
      25 +                                                               
      26 +## Diagnosis                                                   
      27 +                                                               
      28 +### A client gets 429                                          
      29 +                                                               
      30  1. Find the response's `X-Request-Id`, which is also the body's
           `requestId`.
      31  2. Look it up in nginx's access log (`docker compose logs nginx
          `), one JSON line per request with `requestId`, `status`, `upst
          ream` and `upstreamStatus`:
      32     - `status` 429 with no `upstream`: the per-IP limit at nginx
          . The client's address is `remoteAddr`, the TCP peer (SEC-R02),
           never a header it sent.
      33     - `upstreamStatus` 429: a replica answered it, so the per-us
          er limit. The replica's `request completed` line with the same 
          `reqId` has `res.statusCode` 429, and `scf_rate_limited_total` 
          rises.
      34  3. Decide whether the client is misbehaving or the limit is too
           low for a legitimate use. A well-behaved client waits at least
           `Retry-After` before sending more.
      35  
      21 -In AWS, a per-IP block is a 429 from WAF with `Retry-After: 60`
         - and a JSON body of type `/problems/rate-limited` but no `reque
         -stId` (section 1.6 of spec 007); a 403 from WAF is a managed ru
         -le group, not the rate. Both are logged in the log group `aws-w
         -af-logs-scf` with the rule `per-ip-rate-limit` and the client I
         -P. The alarm `scf-waf-blocked-requests` fires above 1000 blocke
         -d requests in 5 minutes; it counts the managed rule groups' blo
         -cks too, so check the rule in the log before blaming the rate. 
         -A per-user 429 shows in the log group `/scf/api` as above.     
      36 +In AWS, a per-IP block is a 429 from WAF with `Retry-After: 60`
         + and a JSON body of type `/problems/rate-limited` but no `reque
         +stId` (section 1.6 of spec 007). A per-user 429 shows in the lo
         +g group `/scf/api` as above.

      70 +### Too many 429s for a legitimate client

           its template from them at start.

           _rps` and apply; the WAF rule's limit is 60 times it.
       77  - In AWS, per user: the service's task definition does not set
            `RATE_LIMIT_USER_MAX` or `RATE_LIMIT_USER_WINDOW_S`, so the d
           efaults apply. Changing them is a change to the `api` containe
           r of the `service` module, deployed like any release ([deploy 
           and migrate](deploy-and-migrate.md)).

         -c.md), LED-R19 to LED-R22). It only reads: it is safe to run at
         - any time, against any database, while the service is serving. 
       3 +What to do when the ledger and the cached balances disagree, or
         + another ledger invariant is violated (section 1.5 of [spec 002
         +](../../specs/002-ledger/spec.md), LED-R19 to LED-R22, [ADR-000
         +6](../adr/0006-double-entry-ledger-with-signed-integer-minor-un
         +its.md), [ADR-0007](../adr/0007-system-accounts-without-a-cache
         +d-balance.md)). `npm run reconcile` checks that every customer 
         +account's cached balance equals the sum of its ledger entries, 
         +and that every currency sums to zero. It only reads: it is safe
         + to run at any time, against any database, while the service is
         + serving.

         +its entries and the balance change in one database transaction,
         + so a drift is a defect, not a race.                           
      20 +

         +nd which withdrawals and transfers are checked against, differs
         + from what the ledger says. The customer can spend money they d
         +o not have, or not spend money they have.                      
      22 +- **A rejected ledger write.** The database refused a write tha

         + answered 500 and wrote nothing (LED-R28). Money stayed correct

      2  
      3  How a replica stops without losing requests, and what to do when
          one did not stop cleanly (section 1.8 of [spec 007](../../specs
         /007-security-ops/spec.md), SEC-R25 to SEC-R28). Docker Compose 
         stops a replica on `docker compose stop` or `down`; ECS stops a 
         task on a deployment, a scale-in or a replacement.
      4  
      5 -## What a clean shutdown looks like                             
      5 +### What a clean shutdown looks like                            
      6  
      7  On SIGTERM or SIGINT, Node runs as process 1 with no shell or np
         m in between, so the signal reaches the service (DEP-R20). The r
         eplica then logs, in order:
      8  
     ...
      13  
      14  A second signal during the shutdown is logged as `signal receiv
          ed during the shutdown: ignored` and changes nothing.
      15  
      16 -## When it exits with code 1                                   
      16 +In AWS, ECS deregisters the task from the ALB target group firs
         +t, which stops new requests and lets the ALB drain it for 35 s 
         +(`deregistration_delay`), then sends SIGTERM.                  
      17  
      18 -- `shutdown timeout reached: destroying the work still in fligh
         -t`, at `warn` with `inFlight`, then `shutdown complete, work wa
         -s cut off`: requests were still running when `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_
         -MS` ended. Their connections were destroyed and their database 
         -transactions rolled back (SEC-R28). Since `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS`
         - is at least `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (SEC-R35), this happens only 
         -when a request outlived its own timeout, or when the clean-up a
         -fter a request timeout, bounded by `statement_timeout`, was sti
         -ll running.                                                    
      19 -- `the shutdown did not end in time: exiting`, at `error` with 
         -`limitMs`: a backstop fired at `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` + `SHU
         -TDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` + 5000 ms (37 s with the defaults), because t
         -he shutdown itself hung, for example while closing a connection
         -.                                                              
      20 -- `startup failed`, or a configuration error naming variables: 
         -not a shutdown; the replica never started (SEC-R40).           
      18 +## Symptoms and alerts

      22 -What to do: money stays correct, because a cut-off movement rol

         - the stored response or a fresh run (spec 005). Locally, confir

         -iliation.md)); it runs locally and in CI only, and in AWS has n

         -rn` lines `service unavailable` with `cause` `RequestTimeout` j

         - every deployment is a defect to report.

      25 +Locally, `docker compose ps -a` shows `Exited (1)` or `Exited (

      26 -Exit code 137 means the replica was killed with SIGKILL before 
         -it could finish. Docker Compose waits `stop_grace_period` (40 s
         - for `api-1` and `api-2`) and ECS waits the container's `stopTi
         -meout` (40 s), both above `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` + `SHUTDOWN
         -_TIMEOUT_MS` (32 s) and, with the defaults, above the 37 s back

      27 +## Impact                                                      
      28  
      28 -## Checking a shutdown                                         
      29 +- **Exit code 0.** None: every request was answered.           
      30 +- **Exit code 1, work cut off.** The requests still running wer
         +e cut off: their connections were destroyed and their database 
         +transactions rolled back (SEC-R28). A client retrying with the 
         +same key gets either the stored response or a fresh run (spec 0
         +05), so money stays correct.                                   
      31 +- **Exit code 137.** The replica was killed with SIGKILL before
         + it could finish, so its requests in flight were lost to their 
         +clients as connection errors or 502s; they retry with the same 
         +key, with the same guarantee.                                  
      32  
      30 -Locally:                                                       
      33 +## Diagnosis                                                   
      34  
      35 +1. The exit code and the reason. Locally:                      
      36 +                                                               
      37 +   ```sh                                                       
      38 +   docker compose ps -a api-1          # the exit code is in ST
         +ATUS: Exited (0)                                               
      39 +   docker compose logs api-1 | tail    # the lines above, and t
         +he ones below                                                  
      40 +   ```                                                         
      41 +                                                               
      42 +   In AWS, the task's exit code and `stoppedReason`, and the li
         +nes in `/scf/api`:                                             
      43 +                                                               
      44 +   ```sh                                                       
      45 +   aws ecs describe-tasks --cluster <cluster> --tasks <task-arn
         +> \                                                            
      46 +     --query 'tasks[0].{reason: stoppedReason, exitCode: contai
         +ners[0].exitCode}'                                             
      47 +   ```                                                         
      48 +                                                               
      49 +2. Read the last lines the replica wrote:                      
      50 +   - `shutdown timeout reached: destroying the work still in fl
         +ight`, at `warn` with `inFlight`, then `shutdown complete, work
         + was cut off`: requests were still running when `SHUTDOWN_TIMEO
         +UT_MS` ended. Since `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` is at least `REQUEST_
         +TIMEOUT_MS` (SEC-R35), this happens only when a request outlive
         +d its own timeout, or when the clean-up after a request timeout
         +, bounded by `statement_timeout`, was still running.           
      51 +   - `the shutdown did not end in time: exiting`, at `error` wi
         +th `limitMs`: a backstop fired at `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` + `
         +SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` + 5000 ms (37 s with the defaults), becaus
         +e the shutdown itself hung, for example while closing a connect
         +ion. A `failed to close a resource` line names the resource.   
      52 +   - `uncaught error: shutting down`, at `fatal`: an uncaught e
         +xception or rejection ran the same shutdown and exits 1. Its `e
         +rr` is the defect.                                             
      53 +   - `invalid configuration` or `startup failed`: not a shutdow
         +n; the replica never started (SEC-R40). See [capacity](capacity
         +.md#diagnosis).                                                
      54 +   - No line at all after the last request, and exit code 137: 
         +the process was killed. Docker Compose waits `stop_grace_period
         +` (40 s for `api-1` and `api-2`) and ECS waits the container's 
         +`stopTimeout` (40 s), both above `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` + `S
         +HUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` (32 s) and, with the defaults, above the 37
         + s backstop too, so a shutdown never reaches the kill (DEP-R20,
         + DEP-R27). A 137 means the container ran out of memory (`stoppe
         +dReason` `OutOfMemoryError`, the alarm `scf-ecs-memory`) or a c
         +hanged setting broke that order.                               
      55 +3. For work cut off, find the requests: the `warn` lines `servi
         +ce unavailable` with `cause` `RequestTimeout` just before the s
         +hutdown, and the [timeouts runbook](timeouts-and-503.md) for wh
         +y they were slow.                                              
      56 +                                                               
      57 +## Mitigation                                                  
      58 +                                                               
      59 +1. Money needs nothing: a cut-off movement rolls back, and a re
         +try with the same key replays or runs it once.                 
      60 +2. A shutdown that cut work off once, during an incident, needs
         + nothing else. One that does it on every deployment is a defect
         + to report, with the `inFlight` count and the slow requests.   
      61 +3. An out-of-memory kill: see [capacity](capacity.md#scf-ecs-me
         +mory).                                                         
      62 +4. An uncaught error: roll back the release that brought it if 
         +it repeats ([deploy and migrate](deploy-and-migrate.md#rollback
         +)) and report it with its `err`.                               
      63 +5. A setting that broke the order: restore it. The rules for ch
         +anging the shutdown settings:                                  
      64 +   - `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` (0 to 60000) and `SHUTDOWN_TIMEO
         +UT_MS` (1 to 120000, not less than `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`) are re
         +ad at startup; an invalid value stops the replica before it lis
         +tens (SEC-R40).                                                
      65 +   - Their sum must stay below `stop_grace_period` (40 s) in `c
         +ompose.yaml` and below `stopTimeout` (40 s) in the Terraform (D
         +EP-R20, DEP-R27); the Terraform variables refuse `shutdown_drai
         +n_delay_ms` + `shutdown_timeout_ms` + 5000 ms, the backstop, th
         +at is not below `stop_timeout_seconds`, so the backstop always

         +st_timeout_ms` (SEC-R35). Going beyond means raising `stopTimeo
         +ut` and `stop_grace_period` as well.

  ⎿  Updated docs/runbooks/shutdown.md (+1 -3)
       2  
       3  How a replica stops without losing requests, and what to do whe
          n one did not stop cleanly (section 1.8 of [spec 007](../../spe
          cs/007-security-ops/spec.md), SEC-R25 to SEC-R28). Docker Compo
          se stops a replica on `docker compose stop` or `down`; ECS stop
          s a task on a deployment, a scale-in or a replacement.
       4  
       5 -### What a clean shutdown looks like                           
       6 -                                                               
       7 -On SIGTERM or SIGINT, Node runs as process 1 with no shell or n
         -pm in between, so the signal reaches the service (DEP-R20). The
         - replica then logs, in order:                                  
       5 +**A clean shutdown.** On SIGTERM or SIGINT, Node runs as proces
         +s 1 with no shell or npm in between, so the signal reaches the 
         +service (DEP-R20). The replica then logs, in order:            
       6  
       7  1. `shutting down on SIGTERM: readiness answers 503`. From now 
          on `/health/ready` answers 503 and `/health/live` still answers
           200 (SEC-R26). The replica keeps serving for `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_D
          ELAY_MS` (2000 ms), so the load balancer stops sending it new r
          equests first.
       8  2. `stopped accepting connections`. New connections are refused
           and idle keep-alive connections are closed. A request that sti
          ll arrives on a kept-alive connection is answered 503 `/problem
          s/service-unavailable` with `Connection: close` (its `service u
          navailable` line has `cause` `ShuttingDown`), and the client re
          tries it with the same `Idempotency-Key`.

⏺ Write(docs/runbooks/idempotency-cleanup.md)

       2  
       3  `npm run idempotency:cleanup` deletes the idempotency keys whos
          e TTL has passed (IDM-R22, section 1.4 of [spec 005](../../spec
          s/005-idempotency/spec.md)). It only bounds the size of the `id
          empotency_keys` table: a key expires at its TTL whether or not 
          the cleanup ran, because a request with an expired key replaces
           the row and runs as a new request (IDM-R21).
       4  
       5 -## When it runs                                                
       5 +It runs:                                                       
       6  
       7 -- In AWS, every hour, as a scheduled task (DEP-R37, phase 12-in
         -fra).                                                          
       8 -- Locally, by hand, when the development database has grown, or
         - to check the command after a change.                          
       9 -- Never inside the service: replicas never race on it.         
       7 +- in AWS, every hour, as the scheduled task `scf-idempotency-cl
         +eanup` (DEP-R37), through RDS Proxy as `scf_app`, logging to `/
         +scf/idempotency-cleanup`;                                      
       8 +- locally, by hand, when the development database has grown, or
         + to check the command after a change;                          
       9 +- never inside the service, so replicas never race on it.

         -deletes only expired rows, and skips a row that a request in pr

         -it; that row goes on the next run.                             
      11 +It is safe to run at any time while the service is serving. It 
         +deletes only expired rows, and skips a row that a request in pr
         +ogress holds (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`) instead of waiting for 
         +it; that row goes on the next run. It deletes in batches of 100
         +0 rows, oldest expiry first, each batch in its own database tra
         +nsaction, until a batch deletes fewer than 1000. Each transacti
         +on raises its statement timeout to 600000 ms (10 minutes, SEC-R
         +48). It waits at most 10 seconds to connect.

      22 +One failed run has no effect on clients: keys expire at their T
         +TL either way, and the next hour's run deletes what this one le
         +ft. Runs that keep failing let the table grow by one TTL of tra
         +ffic per hour, which costs storage and makes the key insert of 
         +every movement slower over time. Nothing is ever lost: an expir
         +ed key that stays in the table is replaced by the next request 
         +that uses it.

      151 +The situations below have a runbook but no deployed alarm. Sec
          +tion 1.8 of [spec 008](../specs/008-deployment/spec.md) fixes 
          +the CloudWatch alarms as exactly those above, the local Promet
          +heus has no alert rules, and in AWS nothing scrapes the replic
          +as' metrics yet (section 6 of spec 008). Each row names the si
          +gnal that already exists and the condition to watch it for; th
          +e thresholds are starting points, to tune against the usual ra
          +te. Until one is deployed, an operator watches it: locally on 
          +the Grafana dashboard or with the expression in Prometheus, in
          + AWS in CloudWatch Logs Insights or the ECS task's exit code, 
          +and in CI through the failing step. Deploying them as alarms i
          +s a follow-up that changes section 1.8 of spec 008.

      168  ## Error reporting
      169  
      170  Off by default ([ADR-0023](adr/0023-optional-observability-off
           -by-default.md), section 1.10 of spec 007). With `SENTRY_DSN` 
           set, each replica reports every request answered 500 `/problem
           s/internal-error` to that Sentry-compatible endpoint, with its
            own small client on Node's `fetch` ([src/platform/error-repor
           ting/](../src/platform/error-reporting/)). Nothing else is rep
           orted: no 4xx, no 503 and no error outside a request.

      756  
      757  ## How this was built
      758  
      759 -Every AI session is logged in [docs/ai/](docs/ai/README.md).  
      759 +I built this service with AI, under a spec-first process. I se
          +t the scope and the rules, decided the architecture and approv
          +ed every change to a spec. Claude Code implemented each phase 
          +from the specs, ADRs and plans in this repository, and an inde
          +pendent review agent audited every phase before it was merged.
          + Every session's prompts and responses are in [docs/ai/](docs/
          +ai/README.md), which also explains what the AI did and what I 
          +decided.                                                      
      760  
      761  ## Troubleshooting
      762  
  ⎿  Updated docs/ai/README.md (+21 -1)
      14  5. `/export` saves the transcript to `transcripts/`.
      15  6. `/ship` runs the quality gates, commits and pushes the phase
           branch. When a phase closes, it also opens the pull request to
           `main`, which I merge once CI is green.
      16  
      17 +## What the AI did and what I decided                          
      18 +                                                               
      19 +**How I worked.** I treated the AI as the implementation team a
         +nd kept the decisions. Each phase started from a written spec w
         +ith acceptance criteria, and from an ADR for each significant c
         +hoice. Claude Code implemented it test-first under the rules of
         + AGENTS.md. An independent agent that had not seen the session 
         +audited the result, and /ship committed it only after the quali
         +ty gates passed. Any change to an approved spec needed my appro
         +val, recorded with its date in section 11 of plan 000.         
      20 +                                                               
      21 +**What the AI did.**                                           
      22 +- Claude Code wrote the code, the tests, the Terraform, the CI 
         +pipeline and the documentation, following the specs.           
      23 +- The /audit agent reviewed each phase against the specs and AD
         +Rs; I decided which findings were fixed before shipping.       
      24 +- A separate Claude chat helped me plan the phases, draft the p
         +rompts I gave Claude Code (all of them are in the transcripts),
         + and review each result and each audit before I approved it.   
      25 +                                                               
      26 +**What I decided.** The main decisions:                        
      27 +- PostgreSQL as the only source of truth, although the challeng
         +e allowed in-memory storage: money needs transactions, row lock
         +s and constraints shared by several replicas ([ADR-0005](../adr
         +/0005-postgresql-as-the-only-source-of-truth.md)).             
      28 +- A modular monolith with hexagonal modules instead of microser
         +vices: one transactional boundary for money, with seams that al
         +low a later split ([ADR-0002](../adr/0002-modular-monolith.md),
         + [ADR-0003](../adr/0003-hexagonal-architecture-with-tactical-dd
         +d.md)).                                                        
      29 +- A double-entry ledger in integer minor units, and idempotency
         + inside the same transaction as the movement ([ADR-0006](../adr
         +/0006-double-entry-ledger-with-signed-integer-minor-units.md), 
         +[ADR-0009](../adr/0009-idempotency-inside-the-movements-transac
         +tion.md)).                                                     
      30 +- Two replicas behind a load balancer from the start, so that c
         +oncurrency and retries are tested across processes, not assumed
         +.                                                              
      31 +- AWS on ECS Fargate with RDS, described in Terraform that CI v
         +alidates against policies and that this repository never applie
         +s ([ADR-0014](../adr/0014-aws-deployment-on-ecs-fargate-with-rd
         +s-postgresql.md), [ADR-0015](../adr/0015-terraform-for-infrastr
         +ucture-as-code.md)).                                           
      32 +- Dashboards and error reporting as opt-in extras, off by defau
         +lt, and no product-analytics tool for a money service ([ADR-002
         +3](../adr/0023-optional-observability-off-by-default.md)). No d
         +emo UI, to spend the time on the backend.                      
      33 +                                                               
      34 +**How I checked.** Beyond the gates and the audits, I exercised
         + the running stack by hand (Swagger UI, curl scenarios, Grafana
         +) and reviewed every result with the advisor chat before approv
         +ing it. When CI failed, I asked for the cause before the fix: f
         +or example, the load test's 503s on a two-CPU CI runner came fr
         +om capacity, so CI runs it at 100 requests per second while the
         + local benchmark stays at 200.                                 
      35 +

           nswers 503 `/problems/service-unavailable` with `Retry-After:

           EADME.md#idempotency-key) recommends for a 503, gets the store
           d answer if it did, or runs the request again if it did not.

          -d.tf` is an empty `s3` block; give it a bucket with versioning
          - and S3's native locking, for example `terraform init -backend
          --config="bucket=<state-bucket>" -backend-config="key=supercool
          --finances/terraform.tfstate" -backend-config="region=<region>"
          - -backend-config="encrypt=true" -backend-config="use_lockfile=
          -true"`. Copy `example.tfvars` to a variables file of the envir
          -onment.                                                       
      191 +1. Configure the state backend outside the repository. `backen
          +d.tf` is an empty `s3` block; give it a bucket with versioning
          + and S3's native locking, for example `terraform init -backend
          +-config="bucket=<state-bucket>" -backend-config="key=supercool
          +-finances/terraform.tfstate" -backend-config="region=<region>"
          + -backend-config="encrypt=true" -backend-config="use_lockfile=
          +true"`. Copy `example.tfvars` to `terraform.tfvars`, which Ter
          +raform loads on its own (or pass `-var-file=<file>` to every c
          +ommand below), and fill in its values.                        
      192  2. Create the secrets first, because the cache module reads th
           e Redis token while it is applied: `terraform apply -target=mo
           dule.secrets`.
      193  3. Put every value of the table of the Secrets section with `a
           ws secretsmanager put-secret-value --secret-id <name> --secret
           -string <value>`, generating passwords and tokens with `aws se
           cretsmanager get-random-password --exclude-punctuation --passw
           ord-length 40`. The Redis secret's `url` cannot be known yet: 
           put `{"auth_token": "<token>", "url": "pending"}`.
      194  4. Create the certificate alone, `terraform apply -target=modu
           le.edge.aws_acm_certificate.this`, and publish the DNS records
            of the output `certificate_validation_records` in the domain'
           s zone. AWS refuses an HTTPS listener whose certificate is not
            issued, so this comes before the edge is applied.
      195  5. Apply everything but the service, so that no task starts be
           fore the schema exists: `terraform apply -target=module.networ
           k -target=module.edge -target=module.database -target=module.c
           ache -target=module.observability`. The edge waits on `aws_acm
           _certificate_validation` until ACM has issued the certificate,
            then creates the HTTPS listener with it.
      196  6. Write the Redis `url` from the output `redis_primary_endpoi
           nt`, with the same token, and point the domain at `alb_dns_nam
           e`.
      197 -7. Create the ECR repository, `terraform apply -target=module.

          -me stage for the `cpu_architecture` of the variables and push

      197 +7. Create the ECR repository, `terraform apply -target=module.

           _definition.migrate`.
      199  9. Run the bootstrap task once (below). It creates `scf_owner`

           ds of their secrets, grants `scf_app` to `scf_owner` `WITH ADM
           IN TRUE, INHERIT FALSE, SET FALSE` and gives the database to `
           scf_owner`, as `docker/postgres/init/01-databases.sql` does lo
           cally (section 1.7 of spec 008, ADR-0018). The RDS master cred
           entials are used for nothing else.

          +trap. To run the idempotency cleanup by hand, use `--task-defi
          +nition scf-idempotency-cleanup` with `securityGroups=[<tasks-s
          +g>]`: it connects through RDS Proxy, which the `one-off-db` gr
          +oup cannot reach. A failure prints one line in the task's log 
          +group (`/scf/migrate` or `/scf/bootstrap`) with the SQLSTATE o
          +r the variable at fault, never a password.                    
      218  
      219  ### Every deployment
      220  
      221  The pipeline runs, in order, and stops at the first failure:
      222  
      223 -1. Build and push the image with a new, immutable tag.        
      223 +1. Build and push the image with a new, immutable tag, for the
          + platform of `cpu_architecture`, as in step 7 of the first dep
          +loyment.                                                      
      224  2. Register the migration task definition of that tag: `terraf
           orm apply -var image_tag=<tag> -target=module.service.aws_ecs_
           task_definition.migrate`.
      225  3. Run the migration task and require exit code 0, as `depends
           _on` does locally. Migrations are expand-then-contract ([ADR-0
           020](../adr/0020-expand-then-contract-migrations.md)), so the 
           running version keeps working on the new schema, and readiness
            accepts migrations newer than the code (SEC-R24). A failed mi
           gration stops the deployment with the old version still servin
           g.
      226  4. Apply with the new tag: `terraform apply -var image_tag=<ta
           g>`. ECS starts the new tasks, waits for the ALB to see them h
           ealthy, then deregisters and drains the old ones: each gets SI
           GTERM, keeps serving for `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS`, finishes i
           ts requests within `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` and is killed only af
           ter the 40 s `stopTimeout`.
     ...
      271  
      272  ### Rotating a secret
      273  
      274 -- `JWT_SECRET`, `CURSOR_SECRET`: put the new value, then `aws 
          -ecs update-service --cluster <cluster> --service scf-api --for
          -ce-new-deployment`. Tokens and cursors signed with the old val
          -ue stop being accepted.                                       
      275 -- The owner role's password (`scf/db-owner`): put the new valu
          -e and run the bootstrap task, which sets both roles' passwords
          - to their secrets' current values. Only the migration and boot
          -strap tasks use it, so nothing is interrupted.

  ⎿  Updated docs/adr/0016-error-model.md (+1 -1)
      47  
      48  Chosen option: **Option A**. Every error is RFC 9457 problem de
          tails with a `requestId`. 400 means the request itself is malfo
          rmed (JSON that does not parse, a missing or malformed required
           header, an invalid cursor); 422 means it was understood and re
          fused (validation errors with one entry per field, and money mo
          vements refused by a business rule); 409 means it conflicts wit
          h the current state of the resource it acts on (status changes,
           an already reversed transaction, an idempotency key still in p
          rogress). A single 400 for everything is simpler, but clients c
          ould not tell a bug in their request from a business refusal.
      49  
      50 -The rule and the catalogue are in section 1 and section 4 of sp
         -ec 000 (SYS-R24 to SYS-R29), with each capability spec adding i
         -ts own types. Other codes follow from the same principles: 401 
         -with one body for every authentication failure (AUT-R06); 403 f
         -or a role not permitted, since which operations exist is public
         -, and 404 for a resource the caller may not see, so a foreign a
         -ccount is indistinguishable from an unknown one (SYS-R04, SYS-R
         -05); 413, 415 and 429 from spec 007; transient conditions answe
         -red as section 4 of spec 000 lists them (409 `/problems/request

         - (SYS-R34); 500 `/problems/internal-error` only for a defect (S

         -cy-key-reused` is a refusal of the key, not a business rule, an

  ⎿  Updated docs/adr/0018-two-database-roles.md (+1 -1)
      47  
      48  Chosen option: **Option A**. An owner role runs the migrations 
          and owns the tables; the service connects with a runtime role t
          hat holds only the privileges it needs (`SELECT` and `INSERT` o
          n the ledger), so a bug or a compromised service cannot disable
           the database checks. Triggers reject `UPDATE`, `DELETE` and `T
          RUNCATE` on the ledger even for the owner; only DDL or a superu
          ser could bypass them, and the service never connects as either
          . One shared role is simpler but lets the application turn off 
          its own safety net.

✻ Churned for 40s · done 12:34 PM

❯ /audit                                                                        
  ⎿  Findings, most severe first. The only code change on this branch is the SEC-R57 lost-connection handling. It is correct and fully tested. What needs fixing is documentation that wasn't updated to match it.

     1. Medium: the API reference and the 503 runbook still say a 503 commits nothing unless the request timed out after COMMIT
     - Where:
       - src/platform/http/docs.ts:59
       - src/platform/http/api-reference.ts:61 and :107, and so the exported docs/api/openapi.yaml (around lines 55–60 and 167–175)
       - docs/runbooks/timeouts-and-503.md:3 and :19
       - README.md:779
     - What is wrong: SEC-R57, spec 000 section 4 and table 1.3 of spec 005 say a 503 from a connection lost during COMMIT also has an unknown outcome. These texts don't:
       - The OpenAPI "Stored for replay" cell for /problems/service-unavailable says "no, except at the request timeout after the COMMIT was sent".
       - The type's description says "Nothing is stored, and the transaction rolls back; only when the request timeout comes after the COMMIT was sent does that commit finish".
       - The errors intro says "one not stored committed nothing … except a 503 at the request timeout …".
       - The runbook's intro and Impact section give the request timeout as "the one exception", which contradicts its own ConnectionLost row at line 134.
       - The README troubleshooting row leaves lost connections out of its list of 503 causes.
     - How to trigger it: fail the database over, or run pg_terminate_backend on the backend, while a withdrawal's COMMIT is in flight. The client gets 503 /problems/service-unavailable, and the commit may have succeeded.
     - Impact: a client built from the OpenAPI contract can read that 503 as "nothing committed" and resend with a new Idempotency-Key, which applies the movement twice. The same-key retry advice in the description reduces the risk, but the contract is wrong about an idempotency outcome. AGENTS.md section 10 also requires OpenAPI and README to match the code.
     - Suggested fix:
       - Add "a database connection lost while a statement ran (SEC-R57)" to the causes in api-reference.ts:107.
       - Change the three exceptions in api-reference.ts:61 and :107 and in docs.ts:59 to "the request timeout after COMMIT was sent, or a connection lost during COMMIT".
       - Run npm run openapi:export.
       - Make the same change in runbook lines 3 and 19, and add "a lost database connection" to README line 779.
     - AC violated: SEC-R57 (SEC-AC48), SYS-R34; docs drift.

     2. Low: docs:check crashes on a malformed percent-escape in a link anchor
     - Where: scripts/docs-links.ts:202
     - What is wrong: the path part is decoded inside a try (line 179), but the anchor goes through decodeURIComponent(anchor) unguarded.
     - How to trigger it: any tracked Markdown link such as [x](docs/guide.md#100%).
     - Impact: an uncaught URIError ends the script with a stack trace instead of a file:line failure. CI still fails, but the message doesn't point at the file.
     - Suggested fix: wrap the anchor decode the same way as the path and report "the anchor is not valid percent-encoding".
     - AC violated: DEP-R47 / DEP-AC35 (failures reported per file and line).

     3. Low: demo-env prints account ids for eval without checking they are UUIDs
     - Where: scripts/demo-env.ts:31 (the comment at :90 assumes they are)
     - What is wrong: the seed output schema accepts any string for accounts[].id, but the comment says "Every value is a JWT or a UUID, so single quotes need no escaping". The output is meant for eval "$(make demo-env)".
     - How to trigger it: a seed or API response whose EUR account id contains ' ends up in the shell assignment and is executed.
     - Impact: a local tool only, fed by the project's own API, so the risk is small. Still, it relies on an assumption it never checks.
     - Suggested fix: use z.uuid() for id, and z.enum([...]) for currency.
     - AC violated: DEP-R48 (missing AC for the validation itself).

     Deferred, already planned: a scheduled reconcile task in AWS (docs/deployment/aws.md limitations); accepting a previous JWT_SECRET or CURSOR_SECRET during rotation, and revoking one user's tokens (left out by spec 006 section 6); an operator endpoint to list a customer's accounts or read audit records; deploying the documented alerts that have no alarm yet (db-pool-exhausted, task-exit-unclean).

     - Spec conformance: no findings. SEC-R57, SEC-AC48/49, DEP-R47/48/49 and DEP-AC35 to AC38 are all owner-approved in plan 000 section 11, and their tasks are ticked with tests named by ID.
     - Money: no findings. The Postman collection and requests.http send amounts as digit strings; "10.50" appears only as a deliberate invalid example.
     - Transactions and concurrency: no findings. A lost connection is never retried. No statement is sent after the client reports the loss. A loss known only from the error, such as 57P01, gets a ROLLBACK that then fails. The client is always released with an error, so the pool destroys it.
     - Idempotency (behaviour): no findings. The 5xx is not stored, the key row rolls back with the transaction, and the integration test checks that the retry runs once and the next request replays.
     - Tests: no findings. Unit fakes stand in for the connection loss, and the integration test waits for the backend to block (waitUntilBlocked) instead of sleeping.

     Verdict: fix before shipping

❯ Fix the three findings as the audit suggests. For 1, run npm run              
  openapi:export afterwards so the OpenAPI test passes. For 2 and 3, add a unit 
  test each that fails first (a link with a malformed percent-escape in its     
  anchor; a seed output whose account id is not a UUID). Then run npm run check

  run docs:check reports 0 failures on 105 files, with the new runbooks
  included. Nothing is committed.

  1. 503 after a lost connection. The API reference, the 503 runbook and the
  README now say that a connection lost during COMMIT also has an unknown
  outcome, like the request timeout after COMMIT. The changed texts are in
  docs.ts, api-reference.ts, timeouts-and-503.md (intro and Impact) and README's
  troubleshooting row. I ran npm run openapi:export; its large diff is just the
  new wording, which appears on every route.

  2. docs:check crash. scripts/docs-links.ts now decodes the anchor inside a
  try, like the path. A malformed one is reported at its file and line as "the
  anchor is not valid percent-encoding". The new test in
  test/unit/docs-links.test.ts failed first with the URIError.

  3. demo-env ids. scripts/demo-env.ts now accepts only UUID account ids and the
  five supported currencies. A malformed seed output prints nothing on stdout
  and exits 1. The new case in test/unit/deployment/demo-env.test.ts uses an id
  containing '; touch pwned; ', and it failed first because that id was printed
  for eval.

  The new tests follow their files' existing naming: the link test uses DEP-R47,
  and the demo-env case sits inside the DEP-AC36 test, whose name now ends
  "failed, incomplete or malformed seed". docs/traceability.md lists only test
  files, so it doesn't change. npm run trace covers all 244 ACs, though its
  integration and e2e results come from earlier runs; I re-ran only the unit
  tests.

  One small inaccuracy remains outside the audit's scope. The OpenAPI errors
  intro in src/platform/http/docs.ts still says "title and detail are fixed per
  type", but malformed-request has four different details. I fixed the same
  sentence in the API guide earlier and can align docs.ts too if you want.
